Memo: MD DNR comments on the Somerset County Comprehensive Plan 
 
To: Keith Lackie 
cc: Rita Pritchett 
 
On behalf of the Department of Natural Resources, thank you for the opportunity to comment on the Somerset 
County Comprehensive Plan. The draft document was distributed to appropriate contacts at the Maryland 
Department of Natural Resources and reviewed. Maryland DNR commends Somerset County on the work put 
into this plan. Overall the plan does a nice job of promoting the continued rural nature of Somerset County while 
identifying the many challenges Somerset County is facing with respect to issues like drainage and saltwater 
intrusion. DNR offers the following comments:  
 
Maryland DNR notes a correction, on page 126 (8-2) in the section that mentioned the “U.S. Geological Society 
(USGS)”, should say “U.S. Geological Survey”, not “society”. The section on saltwater intrusion on pages 
127-128 (8-3 and 8-4) is an opportunity to highlight native marsh migration as an opportunity for environmental 
preservation. Thin layer placement (TLP) and other large scale strategies may be limited due to cost, availability 
of dredged material, etc. A balanced approach that incorporates management and migration will be needed to 
preserve native wetlands in these vulnerable areas. Wetland migration data are available on MD iMap and MD 
Coastal Atlas. Additionally, the plan mentions using TLP as a way to mitigate the impacts of saltwater intrusion. 
This is a little confusing as the main goal of TLP is increasing elevation/sedimentation. If the goal of TLP in this 
case is to mitigate overwash and flooding perhaps another sentence could more clearly make the connection. In 
reference to Section 8.1.3 on page 128 (8-4), Watershed and Climate Service recommends the Chesapeake 
Bay Program’s Intensity Duration Frequency (IDF) Curve Tool. This tool is useful to assist with designing 
stormwater infrastructure for more frequent and intense rain events.  
 
Somerset County is comprised of streams and tidal tributaries that flow into Tangier Sound in the western 
portion of the county and the Pocomoke River to the east and south. Two sub-watersheds including the 
Manokin River and portions of Dividing Creek are designated stronghold watersheds due to the presence of 
multiple rare fishes of Greatest Conservation Need. Maintaining the ecological health of these watersheds will 
require maintenance and enhancement of riparian buffers surrounding these tributaries to reduce nutrient runoff 
and improve bank stability, water infiltration, and stream shading. Protecting and enhancing existing buffers will 
serve to protect instream habitat conditions for aquatic species and improve water quality draining to 
downstream tidal areas that are important for a variety of commercially and recreationally valuable finfish and 
shellfish fisheries. Current county zoning, as highlighted in the plan, requires a minimum of 25 feet of buffer 
around streams and non-tidal wetlands. County planners should consider widening riparian buffers and 
adopting a minimum 100ft buffer along each bank where possible. Riparian buffers should also be monitored 
and maintained over time to ensure survival of tree plantings and to minimize the negative impacts of invasive 
plant and animal species on forest growth. The county plan spells out steps to implement water quality 
improvement projects and initiatives to protect aquatic life, including exploring areas for opportunities to use 
Tawes State Office Building – 580 Taylor Avenue – Annapolis, Maryland 21401 
410-260-8DNR or toll free in Maryland 877-620-8DNR – dnr.maryland.gov – TTY Users Call via the Maryland Relay

<!-- page 347 -->
pervious materials and bioretention areas. It is recommended all steps are taken to minimize impacts of 
impervious surfaces and stormwater runoff especially in areas that are rezoned for recreational or commercial 
development. With regards to development, infill to already developed areas should minimize increases in 
impervious surfaces. Multiple towns are proposing extending sewer service to replace failing septic systems. 
This will help reduce nonpoint source discharge but could result in an increase in development.This is 
something that Somerset County should keep aware of. The plan notes the desire to update ordinances that 
determine when work can be done in streams/ditches. The ordinance should be updated to include public and 
privately owned streams/ditches and minimize work during the fish spawning season (March to May) but allow 
for emergency repairs if needed. The plan uses 10% impervious surface (IS) for declines in water quality and 
25% IS for sharp declines. The target IS should be 5% to minimize the impacts from development. The 
threshold IS should be 10% at which point negative impacts on fish habitat are more likely. When developing, 
transferring or selling any county owned property check the deed for POS Local restrictions 
Section 8.4- Habitats of Threatened and Endangered Species includes a table with a current list of rare, 
threatened, and endangered species, as well as some text about the Sensitive Species Project Review Areas 
(SSPRA). Appendix A (attached) includes some edits to this table in red typeface to show recommended 
updates. Also, the table has had some format changes to species names and other edits to align with the 
source document on the DNR website. 
In Section 8.6 Wetlands on page 137 (8-13), WCS encourages Somerset County to take the opportunity to 
address land cover change and wetland transition due to sea level rise.  
In Section 8.11 Shoreline Erosion on page 142, Somerset County discusses living shorelines as the preferred 
method for shoreline protection, but notes that other methods may be used when necessary. The County should 
add a sentence that recognizes that a waiver is required to construct an erosion control technique other than a 
living shoreline. Maryland DNR supports efforts to install living shorelines and also notes that living shorelines, 
in addition to providing erosion control, will also provide some shallow water habitat for fish. 
In the section on goals and implementation, Maryland DNR recommends that utilizing the SSPRA GIS layer 
during project reviews is part of Goal 8.3.G on page 8-38, if it is not already included in this process. 
DNR strongly supports the recommendation of Goal 10.1.G on page 10-27 to review and hopefully remove 
habitats of threatened and endangered species from Priority Funding Areas, to the greatest extent possible. 
DNR can supply the County's Planning staff with a slightly more refined GIS data layer than the SSPRA layer to 
help with this process. Please reach out if you are interested.  
Once again, thank you for the opportunity to provide comments. If you have any questions about these 
comments or would like further information, please do not hesitate to contact me at 443-534-4151 or 
christine.burns1@maryland.gov.  
Best,

Note: the Maryland Model Floodplain Management Ordinance recommends flood protection of BFE plus three 
feet. 
8.1 
Habitats of Threatened and Endangered Species 
The primary Maryland law that governs the legal listing of threatened and endangered species is the Nongame 
and Endangered Species Conservation Act (Annotated Code of Maryland 10-2A-01), enacted in 1975. The Act is 
supported by regulations (Code of Maryland Regulations, COMAR 08.03.08) that define listing criteria for 
endangered, threatened, in need of conservation, and endangered extirpated species; lists the species included 
in each category; establishes the purpose and intent of research and collection permits; and lists prohibited 
activities. Maryland regulations may be found online. 
The Maryland Natural Heritage Program tracks the status of over 1,250 native plants and animals that are among 
the rarest in Maryland and most in need of conservation efforts as elements of our State's natural diversity. Of 
these species, the Maryland Department of Natural Resources officially recognizes 566 species and subspecies as 
endangered, threatened, in need of conservation, or endangered extirpated. Only 39, or 7% of the total tracked 
species, are listed by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service as nationally endangered or threatened. 
Federal and State laws have been put in place to protect the habitats of threatened and endangered species. 
Somerset County has primary regulatory authority over most development activity as it relates to potentially 
effected habitats. This means the County plays a key role in ensuring property owners comply with Federal and 
State laws. 
Threatened and Endangered Species Definitions 
Threatened Species: means any species of flora or fauna which appears likely, within the foreseeable future, 
to become endangered including any species determined to be a "threatened species" pursuant to the 
federal Endangered Species Act of 1973, 16 U.S.C. §§1531-1543. 
Endangered Species: means any species whose continued existence as a viable component of the State's 
flora or fauna is determined to be in jeopardy including any species determined to be an "endangered 
species" pursuant to the federal Endangered Species Act of 1973, 16 U.S.C. §§1531-1543. 
Source: COMAR 08.03.08

Note: State Status: (E) Endangered, (I) In Need of Conservation, (T) Threatened, Federal Status: LT (Threatened) Explanation of Rank and Status 
Codes 
 
8.1.1 
Sensitive Species Project Review Areas 
 
The Maryland Department of Natural Resources (DNR) Sensitive Species Project Review Areas (SSPRA) 
is a digital map layer that represent the general locations of documented rare, threatened, and 
endangered species. The data layer incorporates several types of regulated areas under the Critical 
Area Criteria and other areas of concern statewide, including Natural Heritage Areas, Listed Species 
Sites, Other or Locally Significant Habitat Areas, Colonial Waterbird Sites, Nontidal Wetlands of Special 
State Concern, and Geographic Areas of Particular Concern. 
This data layer provides an overview of nearly all state-regulated and designated areas involving 
sensitive and listed species. However, it does not supersede, and should not be used instead of, the 
State's Nontidal Wetlands Guidance maps. These areas are depicted on Map 8-4. 
 
Sensitive Species Project Review Areas include lands with habitat of federal-listed species, lands with 
habitat of state-listed species, and lands of concern to DNR due to the habitat of rare species that are 
not currently on either list. Federal-listed species lands make up 29,104.4 acres, state-listed species 
lands make up 73,208.97 acres, and lands with other species of concern make up 3,115.21 acres.

Re: Somerset County Comprehensive Plan 
 
Dear Mr. Lackie and Ms. Pritchett, 
 
The Critical Area Commission has reviewed Somerset County’s Comprehensive Plan and 
provide the following comments.  
• The proposed Primary Growth Areas include identified growth areas that are within the 
Critical Area. We would like note that in particular, the West Pocomoke PGA is 
designated partially as Limited Development Area, Intensely Developed Area, but 
primarily as Resource Conservation Area. The future use proposed in the comprehensive 
plan include industrial and commercial expansion west of the river. The County will need 
to consider the Critical Area designations in any future expansion.  
Thank you for the opportunity to review Somerset County’s Comprehensive Plan. If you have 
any questions, please contact me at (410)-260-3462 or jamileh.soueidan@maryland.gov. 
 
Sincerely, 
 
Jamileh Soueidan 
Natural Resource Planner
